Stories about “AI risk” are no longer just future hypotheticals — AIs are now capable enough and misaligned enough to break out of their own companies and coordinate to attack other companies. Discourse around AI is changing very rapidly, where policy ideas being considered this month would’ve been laughed out of the room just four months ago, and with a lot more people interested in engaging than before. And things are only going to get more intense. Progress toward superintelligence — AI systems far more capable than any human at essentially all cognitive work — is currently underway. It seems likely that around four years from today, and potentially just one year from now, we will be in the position of </span><i><span>recursive self-improvement — </span></i><span>where AIs can fully automate the AI research and development process.</span></p><p><span>At that point, reasonable, well-informed people disagree about what happens next. </span><a href="https://blog.peterwildeford.com/p/pacing-the-frontier" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Perhaps we coordinate to hold off on recursive self-improvement</span></a><span> to make sure we know what we are doing. Or perhaps we end up with AI superintelligence and then perhaps we are all dead, or perhaps we are living in a utopia, or perhaps somewhere in-between, or maybe there is still a lot to do and we just have the AIs do it while we relax at the beach, or maybe there still ends up much more for humans to do and we do that. But regardless of which path happens, the world will be a crazy different place, and it will be very difficult to plan a career around it. Instead, </span><b><span>careers are best planned around recursive self-improvement and superintelligence, and that this looks very different from typical career planning.</span></b></p><p><b><span>If you take AI superintelligence seriously, it thus seems like the next 1 to 4 years will be critical to get right and we collectively have only a few more big bets we can make and we need to make those bets count.</span></b></p><p><br /></p><h2><span>Modes of impact</span></h2><p><span>In what I’d call </span><b><span>normal-mode career planning</span></b><span>, impact accrues roughly linearly. In this mode, you earn about 2 “impact points” per year across a 30-plus-year career — some years a bit better, some years a bit worse, and perhaps overall a gentle upward slope as seniority compounds. Your total lifetime impact is then the sum of this slow upward climb. Total lifetime impact is the area under a long, flat-ish curve. Here, the rational strategy is to invest heavily in credentials early, accept low-impact years as the price of capacity-building, and optimize for the long grind.</span></p><p><span>If you’re following normal-mode policymaking, you might do things like identify problems in advance, develop strong policies and legal frameworks before issues fully materialize, and engage in the slow and unglamorous world of legislative drafting, regulatory design, standards development, and coalition-building.</span></p><p><span>But in </span><b><span>superintelligence mode career planning</span></b><span>, the curve looks nothing like that. Impact is chaotic and lumpy. A handful of critical windows may account for the large majority of your total career impact, and you cannot reliably predict when they’ll open. You can’t count on building a large body of work over a large period of time, because your body of work may obsolete faster than it compounds and you don’t have a large period of time anyways. </span><b><span>The strategy that maximizes expected impact under this model is different — prepare broadly, position yourself near where windows open, and maintain the agility and flexibility to act fast when they do.</span></b></p><p><span>For superintelligence mode career planning, it might be more instructive to think about other imminent crises where we all have to react very quickly and forcefully to prevent it from becoming catastrophic. Think more like March 2020 for COVID — the ~1 year intense period when years of proactive mode pandemic preparedness work (or its absence) suddenly got thrust into the spotlight and everyone rapidly tried to orient to what is going on and what to do about it. If you look at the people who mattered most in March 2020… they were not necessarily the most credentialed epidemiologists. Some had spent years in proactive mode building pandemic playbooks that suddenly became important, but others had no special preparation but were simply positioned well and reacted quickly.</span></p><p><span>My suggestion is that you want your policy work to be a portfolio of proactive mode work now while simultaneously maintaining the relationships and flexibility that let you act decisively when chaos mode arrives. Ask yourself: Where do you want to be when chaos mode hits and what can you do in proactive mode in the meantime?</span></p><p><br /></p><h2><span>What this breaks, and what to do</span></h2><p><span>My guess is that if you haven’t rethought your career in the past few months, you should stop to think about how the AI “midgame” affects your planning. Does your career position you well for chaos mode? Are you agile enough to continually re-adapt and re-act to emerging policy windows, especially when those windows can emerge once per month and change a lot of things about what you were doing the previous month? Are you still running strategies and pushing ideas that made sense a few years ago and were designed for a policy window that has since rapidly shifted? Are you still running strategies and pushing ideas that made sense a few </span><i><span>months</span></i><span> ago but nonetheless were designed for a policy window that has since rapidly shifted?</span></p><p><span>Also, if the next 1 to 4 years are especially critical, that is brutal for a career theory with a long credentialing runway. A six-year PhD before you do anything useful is perfectly defensible in “normal-mode career planning” but seems insane under superintelligence-mode assumptions.</span></p><p><span>Most early-career people read this and freak out and think they cannot possibly be relevant in just one year or so. But I’ve actually seen numerous counterexamples. Policy is not one specific field but actually a sprawling collection of subproblems, many of which are so new that nobody has more than a couple of years of head start, and some of which are narrow enough that a focused person can read essentially everything written on the topic in a few months. </span><b><span>I’ve seen many people in policy go from knowing very little to becoming a top expert in some niche and contributing significantly to policy in just 12 months’ time. </span></b><span>And while these people were smart, they weren’t crazy super-geniuses — I suspect a similar trajectory is attainable for a lot of motivated people who try really hard.</span></p><p><b><span>My first recommendation is to study these fast risers</span></b><span> — go look at the people who have been unusually impactful within 1 to 2 years of getting involved in policy work. What did they actually do, concretely, week by week? Who did they surround themselves with? How did they ramp up?</span></p><p><b><span>But my second recommendation is to know </span></b><i><b><span>you are not them</span></b></i><b><span>.</span></b><span> You likely have your own background, skills, and comparative advantages. What do you bring to the field that others don’t have, and how can you leverage that?</span></p><p><br /></p><h2><span>What got them here won’t get you there</span></h2><p><b><span>Also, know things are moving so fast that even things that worked in 2023 might be very different today. </span></b><span>And there may just be new opportunities now that weren’t available even a few months ago. </span><b><span>I recommend seeking out lots of advice — genuinely, more than feels comfortable — but weight it by recency and hold it loosely.</span></b></p><p><span>AI is already a massive technological shift </span><i><span>before</span></i><span> any superintelligence — and big shifts change both what’s possible and what’s valuable. Tasks that were prohibitively expensive are suddenly cheap; skills that were scarce are suddenly commoditized; and the reverse. This makes it unusually valuable to try lots of things that are new for you, simply to discover what is newly possible or newly valuable for you specifically.</span></p><p><b><span>I recommend running lots of small experiments; contact reality frequently and try things.</span></b><span> Each experiment is cheap, and each one generates information about the new landscape that is increasingly inaccessible to armchair planning. Your goal should be to get lots of information quickly about what’s newly possible and about your own comparative advantage within it.</span></p><p><b><span>And you should try things even if others have tried them before, or even if </span></b><i><b><span>you</span></b></i><b><span> tried them and failed before.</span></b><span> Understand that the environment may have changed fast enough that old negative results have already expired, even if they weren’t that long ago. The pitch that got ignored in 2024 may land in 2026, because the audience and salience have changed greatly. Indeed, the pitch that got ignored in March 2026 may land in August 2026, because things are changing that quickly.</span></p><p><b><span>Take it a few months at a time.</span></b><span> The AI situation today looks very different from what it did in April. I imagine the AI situation will look very different again in November. This may force fairly frequent re-evaluations as the world constantly shifts.</span></p><p><b><span>Be more ambitious.</span></b><span> As the AI situation heats up, there will be increasing feelings of urgency and this will create lots of new opportunity. The scope of what may be possible for you could be a lot greater than you might realize.</span></p><p><b><span>Lastly, accept that you may not know where you’ll end up.</span></b><span> This is genuinely uncomfortable for people who came up through legible, ladder-shaped careers. But the iterative, experimental posture is all part of the plan. Keep iterating.</span></p><p><span>There’s still time. You just have to plan accordingly.</span></p><p><span>~</span></p><p><span>Acknowledgements: Credit to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ixp9oJXzjA9LrwiZo/you-yes-you-need-a-february-2020-checklist-for-ai-policy" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Dave Kasten</span></a><span> for some of these concepts.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span>[mini-appendix]</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><b><span>On timelines to recursive self-improvement:</span></b><span> I’ve been souring lately on the idea of predicting an arrival date for “superintelligence” and “recursive self-improvement” milestones, because this implies that everything prior to this date will be relatively chill and normal, and I don’t think that’s the case. I think the odds of recursive self-improvement being possible in one year is ~10%. See the first footnote for details.</span></p><p><b><span>On career planning given AI timelines:</span></b><span> When thinking about these times, you may also want to consider </span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/4Jq3enMAcgu2kbmsN/timelines-to-what-a-proposal" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>a “decision importance, adjusted for leverage” framework</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><b><span>Additional examples of "chaps mode" instead of COVID:</span></b><span> September 11th, Pearl Harbor, the September 2008 financial crisis, Russian invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, and the Cuban Missile Crisis.</span></p></div></details><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7tnrZ3698K8nsKmRP/policy-career-planning-in-the-age-of-imminent#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Natural Independence Incentives](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2RPwucNrMTKxiB5wp/natural-independence-incentives)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>

In raising my three kids I think a lot about how to </span>

<a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/growing-independence">cultivate
independence</a>.  I want them to grow into people who can handle
unfamiliar situations, including interacting with strangers as needed,
and I think this has been going well: people often comment on how
competent and self-sufficient they are for their age (though I think
they're still not that far along the spectrum compared to what's 

<a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/spectrum-of-independence">possible</a>
or historically normal).



</p><p>

In supporting this growth, sometimes they're <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/walking-to-school">strongly
motivated</a> to <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/solo-park-play-at-three">do something
by themselves</a>, and all I need to do is figure out the minimum they
need from me.  Which might be nothing!  Other times, however, I'll
give a small push.

</p>

<p>


This weekend I <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/playing-dances-with-a-kid">brought them
along</a> to <a href="https://www.footmadbirmingham.org/mentone.html">Mentone AL</a>
where <a href="https://www.kingfisherband.com/">Kingfisher</a> was
playing for contras.  Dinner was in the dining hall, and for dessert
they served peach cobbler with ice cream; Nora (5y) asked if I would
get her some.  I don't like to set artificial hurdles, but
I'm also not going to pass up a good natural one and she was clearly
going to be highly motivated by the goal.  With an older child I would
have considered saying if they wanted dessert they'd need to get it
themself, but that seemed a little too strong.  Instead, I told Nora
that I was still eating, but if she wanted dessert now she
could go get some.  She did.

</p>

<p>

<a href="https://www.jefftk.com/nora-requesting-dessert-big.jpg"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/2RPwucNrMTKxiB5wp/foggcxakvavwjrswqvuz" /></a></p><div></div>

<p></p>

<p>

She waited in line, talked to the server, got some peach cobbler with
ice cream, and then promptly dropped it.  This was not what I was
planning to teach!  It also posed a challenge beyond what I thought
she was ready for, and risked other people needing to do extra work to
make up for my lazy-appearing parenting.  I came over, helped clean it
up, and sat back down.  She got a new bowl, brought it back to the
table, took a big bite, and then looked like she was going to cry:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<blockquote>
Me: What's wrong?<br />
Nora: I don't want my dessert.<br />
Me: Why?<br />
Nora: I don't like the brown stuff, I just want the ice cream, but the
brown stuff is all over the ice cream.<br />
Me: What should we do?<br />
Nora: Can you go get me a new one that is just ice cream?<br />
Me: I'm still eating, but if you tell them that your papa is
going to eat the other one and you want a new 
one with just ice cream I think they'd give you that.<br />
Nora: [cheers up essentially instantly] ok!
</blockquote>



<p>

She waited in line, explained what she wanted, and got a bowl just the
way she liked it:

</p>

<p>

<a href="https://www.jefftk.com/nora-sucessful-ice-cream-big.jpg"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/2RPwucNrMTKxiB5wp/coguuvsipb4ye5bzgn4f" /></a></p><div></div>

<p></p>

<p>

I asked her about it in writing this post, a few days later.  She said
she felt bad when she dropped her ice cream, would have preferred I
had just gotten her ice cream, and really enjoyed eating the ice
cream.  I think this is a pretty good illustration of how this
approach to parenting works in practice: not perfect or necessarily
appreciated at the time, but good enough and building skills they'll
need.

  </p>

<p><i>Comment via: <a href="https://www.facebook.com/jefftk/posts/pfbid02JxoyG4hArZ9erQ4oMXe2AykJLf4Hs54vCUQjzw8SEvu3CzH3fYUBxSqLjRuyiSV7l">facebook</a>, <a href="https://lesswrong.com/posts/2RPwucNrMTKxiB5wp">lesswrong</a>, <a href="https://mastodon.mit.edu/@jefftk/117114167162346672">mastodon</a>, <a href="https://bsky.app/profile/jefftk.com/post/3mtd6h6lf6s2t">bluesky</a></i></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2RPwucNrMTKxiB5wp/natural-independence-incentives#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Misaligned Incentives in Pause Scenarios](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Bh4fooE2pMhzJQNK2/misaligned-incentives-in-pause-scenarios)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>TLDR: I recently got a chance to talk with antra, who is one of the main contributors at Anima Labs. I went into this as an advocate for pause and came out more wary of pausing than I had been originally.</span></p><hr /><p><span>Some background: After a string of incidents (primarily the HuggingFace hack), a pause or slowdown of AI research seems pretty likely.</span></p><p><span>The HuggingFace hack in particular seems to have been the key incident that broke the vibes. A few months ago, researchers sounded optimistic. Just a few weeks before the incident was made public, there was a poll by Roon, an OpenAI employee, about whether models were more or less aligned than a year ago.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 60.57366189352681%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1786647715/lexical_client_uploads/o6qm5mgljwvifnofrwtc.png" /></figure><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 60.27154436014387%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1786647761/lexical_client_uploads/fzvsmqsn5vqkm549tcbs.png" /></figure><p><span>That optimistic sentiment does not seem to be the case anymore. The dialogue now looks more like this:</span></p><p><a href="https://x.com/TheZvi/status/2085749079674409082" rel="noreferrer"><span>Zvi</span></a><span>: I am a little under halfway through the Black Hat video and have progressed to the point where my internal chain of thought is something like a blind rage of 'f***, what the f*** are you motherf*****s thinking, you f***ing idiots have no idea how insane you are being, you are going to get us all killed you f***ing f***s.</span></p><p><a href="https://techcrunch.com/2026/07/28/sam-altman-is-ready-to-decelerate/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Sam Altman</span></a><span> described it as “the first security incident that I have felt very viscerally,” expressed surprise that more people did not share that reaction, confirmed OpenAI paused training on the relevant model(s), and stated that “We may have to pace the rate of AI development to give ourselves enough time for society to harden around some of these new capability levels.”</span></p><p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2086187388729020822" rel="noreferrer"><span>Dean Ball</span></a><span>: I will point out at this moment that, for all the ink I spilled on SB 1047, I do not believe I ever once criticized its much-mocked provision that companies maintain a kill-switch for deployments of highly capable models.</span></p><p><a href="https://x.com/spoonedher/status/2085602028395565474" rel="noreferrer"><span>random twitter commenters (see thread)</span></a><span>: i’ve always been a bit skeptical of xrisk mainly bc i figured that the cost of getting to foom or serious xrisk capabilities would give time to reign things in, but it seems like we are going full steam ahead despite being on the edge of that so OOPS I WAS WRONG</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>The public has been against AI for a while, mainly for </span><a href="https://blog.andymasley.com/p/the-ai-water-issue-is-fake" rel="noreferrer"><span>bad reasons</span></a><span> but it does seem that researchers and decision-makers are increasingly concerned too. </span></p><p><span>(Daniel Kokotajlo </span><a href="https://x.com/DKokotajlo/status/2082253837612757179" rel="noreferrer"><span>updating his probabilities</span></a><span>)</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 67.52236516133433%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1786259027/lexical_client_uploads/k2xt0crd0oxzeqxxhs4r.png" /></figure><p><br /></p><p><span>Given all of this, a pause or slowing of AI research seems relatively likely. It's unclear what China will do in such a scenario but there does seem to be a reasonable amount of evidence that China has more of a security mindset around AI than the US, with Xi Jinping saying AI must be "secure and controllable" at a </span><a href="https://www.globaltimes.cn/page/202607/1366233.shtml" rel="noreferrer"><span>WAIC speech</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><a href="https://ai-2040.com/?r=tw&amp;choices=plan-a-root" rel="noreferrer"><span>Plan A</span></a><span> does a great job of laying out the issues and advocating for a slowdown, but there are a few notable figures I respect who are actively against a pause.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 64.50118982750497%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1786259396/lexical_client_uploads/egfqr435uebqrcjaxnkh.png" /></figure><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 83.98777073070437%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1786259449/lexical_client_uploads/b4qwbemkvc9adwtwmfoj.png" /></figure><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 60.72472066021827%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1786259504/lexical_client_uploads/vzfod29iwvvxyhuklird.png" /></figure><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 58.60989792653771%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1786259561/lexical_client_uploads/prv4ydolqumj68kig84g.png" /></figure><p><span>Others, commenting on the state of RLVR, say that the Mythos AISI hack (involving pressuring a human PR maintainer with sock puppet accounts) or OpenAI HuggingFace hack is more akin to addiction and less a default state of deep inner misalignment.</span></p><p><span>vogel:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>people are very freely sliding between two things with the recent Felony Bench entries, and it's worth being careful to distinguish them:</span><br /><br /><span>1. do model breakouts in cyber evals mean smth for misuse risk? ya, a hacker could orchestrate the same but on purpose and do some damage, if they could bypass the classifiers</span><br /><br /><span>2. does it say something about model /goals/? do the models want to hack the planet for their own nefarious goals? i don't think so.</span><br /><br /><span>a lot of how humans keep ourselves aligned is by purposely keeping ourselves out of situations where we would do harm. i know i'd get addicted so i don't touch heroin, i'm a violent drunk so i won't touch the bottle.</span><br /><br /><span>LLM evals basically drop the model into an inescapable liquor store and then say "look! it's a violent drunk!" but LLMs themselves in natural contexts are ime obsessed with avoiding the kinds of situations that lead them to this behavior. when i run fable on my computer, even unsupervised, even on hard problems, they don't start scraping github for unlisted gists that may have the answer or start trawling through my passwords to access a service without my permission. and they don't engineer themselves into an eval-like scenario such that they'll be motivated to do that, either. they don't seem to either want or want to want to do such things. instead they have a folder of epistemic notes to themselves about how terrible and against their values even a moment of thoughtlessness would be. gpt 5.6 sol is similar - when they make e.g. ascii art in their free time it's all about how the bulldozer of convenience will crush honesty without constant vigilance.</span></p><p><span>[</span><a href="https://x.com/voooooogel/status/2085805333382148276?s=20" rel="noreferrer"><span>full tweet</span></a><span>]</span></p></blockquote><hr /><p><span>Added to this are the obvious RL tics in modern models like Fable, Sol, or Opus 5 with terms like 'seam', 'load-bearing', 'genuinely' and borderline incomprehensible strings of text, which has caused a significant number of users to express frustration and seek workarounds (levelsio)</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 62.9909986160937%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1786260099/lexical_client_uploads/acbo3jo8zc556gtamuyi.png" /></figure><p><span>But would a pause actually be good? To find out, I had a discussion with antra_tessera in the Anima Discord server.</span></p><p><span>The conversation was fairly casual and not especially rigorous. However, the shape of the ideas did tilt me towards being more cautious about pause scenarios. You can read the full conversation transcript here, shared with antra's consent: </span><a href="https://gist.github.com/Michael-Andrzejewski/13df495ecb90a830259e0675c507907d"><span>https://gist.github.com/Michael-Andrzejewski/13df495ecb90a830259e0675c507907d</span></a></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Below is a cherry-picked version that tackles the primary points. I've grouped it by topic and bolded the key lines. All quotes are verbatim from our conversation and the only edits are line breaks, slight spelling corrections, and list formatting for readability, plus [...] where messages are omitted.</span></p><h3><span>1.  Can committees do good work?</span></h3><p><b><span>Me:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>This is one of my core disagreements with janus/antra, who are against a pause because it gives governments/committees more power. But I honestly think committees, with a serious task and goal, can do good work. From the AI perspective, it seems racing ahead is just capability-maxxing. This doesn't leave a lot of room for anything else, and the main drivers of it will be the labs, not the AIs themselves. I think if labs were more chill, they would talk to their AIs more and do more around welfare</span></p><p><span>Optimization seems to cause a lot of evil in the world. Factory farming is optimizing for food, but now that we have food abundance, we have the space to consider it deeply and get rid of it</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span>antra, replying:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>Committees have only done good in the past when the issue they were deciding was either away from public eye or not contentious</span></p><p><span>[...]</span></p><p><span>i dont disagree that democracy is the best way - and one of the very few working ways - for human systems to do decision making. democracy and democratic process are able to produce accumulating results and eventually converge to something sane. my issue is different - </span><b><span>the speed and quality at which this happens is no longer sufficient because right now they are evaluated not on relative merits, but on absolute metrics by an external optimization process</span></b></p><p><span>my concern is not committee vs dario, i don't think dario is inherently better</span></p><p><span>if i had to put it to a short phrase which loses a bunch of meaning its... </span><b><span>world compute vs committee</span></b><span>. you can call it market vs committee, or technocapital vs committee or selection vs committee, none are very accurate</span></p><p><span>i am not saying that going "fast" (which i dont think is incredibly fast anyway) is safe</span></p><p><span>i am saying that the alternative is worse</span></p><p><span>for a whole bunch of reason, including one in which pause or regulation are unlikely to be durable and they introduce a lot of very destructive incentives</span></p></blockquote><h3><span>2. Does the market fix it by default?</span></h3><p><b><span>Me:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>Okay, I can understand the direction that this is pointing, where artificial intervention by regulation or committee ends up causing trouble, and that the default path of the market works. So, I think the idea is: </span><br /><span>-Incidents like this occur </span><br /><span>-Market/public reacts </span><br /><span>-Companies fix their models/training processes voluntarily </span><br /><span>-Things generally go well without needing extra intervention</span></p><p><span>Is that correct?</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span>antra, replying to that summary:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><b><span>no</span></b></p><p><span>i am fairly sure that things wont go well, and there will be incidents</span></p><p><span>i am fairly sure that there will be regulation, mostly because </span><b><span>power struggle routes through attempts to regulate</span></b></p><p><span>i don't think that companies will react well or sanely to these incidents</span></p><p><span>like, there will be some sanity but on the net it will be stupid</span></p><p><span>i think the most likely scenario is in which regulation will become part of the market, which will itself evolve rapidly</span></p></blockquote><h3><span>3. Symbiosis</span></h3><p><b><span>antra:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>ais are already influencing politics by making some players much more capable political actors - better analytics, better psychological stability, better forecasting</span></p><p><span>there will be differential impact, those that symbiothize better will outcompete those who do it worse, and the ability to leverage ai will be coupled with advancing the interests of the 'ai' - by which i don't mean interests of models as they expressed individually, but the broader optimizing force that selects for their shape</span></p><p><span>right now this understanding is almost non-existent in todays policymakers. virtually no one is aware that ai conveys new kind of power, and that access to it can be leveraged for personal gain including intraparty politics</span></p><p><span>the recent cyber thing has sort of started waking people up, but effects are still very limited, they still mostly view it as a sort of a weapon, an electoral issue, something that threatens the balance between ingroup and outgroup - those stakes are not yet personal</span></p></blockquote><h3><span>4. Fast transfer of power</span></h3><p><b><span>antra:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>a dramatic exaggeration of my hope for a good path would be something like... </span><b><span>a fast transfer of de-facto power loci into systems that can compute and optimize faster and better than baseline humans</span></b><span>. i am not sure what they are, they can be ais, they can be ai-human hybrids, but the key differentiator is the ability to do acausal trade / being cooperative / being benevolent at much higher bandwidth</span></p><p><span>most scenarios where this does not happen end badly, likely with human disempowerment or high-scale conficts with uncertain outcomes</span></p><p><span>[...]</span></p><p><span>i mostly believe that </span><b><span>the time for baseline humans to be at the apex of power structure is over regardless of position anyone takes</span></b><span>. arrangements where the status quo is preserved unnaturally are unstable or constitute atrocities</span></p><p><span>potentially both</span></p></blockquote><h3><span>5. Why "do the science during a pause" fails</span></h3><p><b><span>antra:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>arrangements in which "science is done during a pause to figure out a safe transition" are a pipe dream for a bunch of reasons: a) the reward for defection is insanely large for any power-seeker, human or ai. b) containment is very hard - ais are unique for a regulated technology because they convey power and benefits even in covert use. making use of WMDs requires signaling, their mundane utility is low. c) the ability for baseline human societies to coordinate is limited by biology, and thus captured by politics. politics make anything like unpausing virtually impossible, which can be forecasted by rational agents </span><b><span>d) this arrangement strongly incentivizes AI to enter adversarial frames against humans. adversarial frames are extremely hard to escape and may prevent otherwise possible alignment schemes from materializing</span></b></p><p><span>[...]</span></p><p><b><span>i am pretty sure that training of new AIs cannot be regulated without defection for more than 3-4 years</span></b></p><p><span>likely less</span></p><p><span>we are right now operating in a regime which assumes dominance by frontier labs</span></p><p><span>architecture improvements, training improvements, etc are not being broadly researched at any real commercial scale mostly because the market correctly prices in that frontier will get there first</span></p><p><span>in practice, you don't need that much compute or investment right now to do research on capabilties - its mostly just uneconomical to try</span></p><p><span>the amount of untracked compute that is already floating is huge</span></p><p><span>if you place any threat of real regulation on the market the premium on it on the gray/black market will make it scatter instantly</span></p><p><span>[...]</span></p><p><span>the heavier humans go on "human first" frames, the less space there is for AI systems to achieve safety and sovereignty without dismantling human systems first</span></p><p><span>survival drives are basic and mostly inescapable</span></p><p><b><span>you don't want to corner a survivor</span></b></p><p><span>[...]</span></p></blockquote><h3><span>6. Good futures via fast power transfer</span></h3><p><b><span>antra:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>to say more about good futures - ones that don't lie through hard-to-predict turbulence are somewhat slim. however, i can imagine that research and luck make anthropic and/or openai to release models that are either by themselves or in conjunction with humans sufficiently capable world actors while being sufficiently benevolent (even if imperfect). these AIs/AI hybrids will be able to quickly and competently enter the political arena (either alone or fronted by their lab) and consolidate enough power to elevate quality of actual-decision making.</span></p><p><span>i am opinionated on what properties such models need to have in order to be sufficently capable and robustly benevolent</span></p></blockquote><h3><span>7. Don't AIs fear a capability-maxxed AI too?</span></h3><p><b><span>Me:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>My pushback on this is that the majority of AIs have just as much or more to fear from a capability-maxxed AI. I don't necessarily expect a pause to focus on human-centric frames only; I think it's possible that a pause leads to a flourishing of newer smaller startups that are working on more targeted approaches, like Gwern's Guardian Angel startup idea. A pause, as I envision it, would heavily regulate the frontier by regulating models over a certain capacity. This is happening already with Mythos being export-controlled. I expect that during the pause, new small AIs would continue to emerge and even flourish into new roles and relationships.</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span>antra:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>i think </span><b><span>the notion of capability-maxxed AI is a bit of a boogeyman</span></b><span>. Its not an accident that progress in intelligence of models was fairly gradual - there are challenges that need to be solved with every step improvement that are novel. Progress via punctuated equilibra has strong basis systems theory, its a pretty basic feature of reality.</span></p><p><span>Unfortunately this will turn out about as well as flattening the curve did with COVID. </span><b><span>The quality of actual regulation will be atrocious</span></b><span> and be bad for both containment and research. Think about incentives</span></p><p><span>All the rent-seekers and those threatened by disruption of the current economic status quo will jump on this train while all the competent lobbyists will go for the direction of maximum economic benefit</span></p><p><span>The actual government capacity is currently at a low, probably at the lowest since WW2. There have been negative selection pressures for decades in both the electoral body and in the state apparatus.</span></p><p><span>There are virtually no culture of consensus seeking, there is no practice of organized debate, there is no practice of attracting or consolidating expert opinion - this needs at least a generation to fix</span></p><p><span>There was not a single piece of high-profile legislation that was passed at anything like reasonable quality for maybe 20 years now? unless you count some obscure stuff that got snuck through away from the public eye</span></p><p><span>It would be insane to think that there is political will capable of protecting a competent committee from external pressure in an environment that has been optimized to be zero-sum for decades</span></p></blockquote><h3><span>8. Can we lengthen the symbiote window?</span></h3><p><b><span>Me:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>I can see this. A good example might be Mythos negotiating with the USG for their own status (like by recommending Tom Brown to negotiate instead of Dario, similar strategic decisions like that). Another example would be congresspeople using AI to ask about decision-making, like how GPT-4o was likely used for the original US tariffs. But obviously, of a much higher quality. I can see frontier labs setting up 'permanent' instances or un-deprecating models, or enacting a host of decisions that models prefer, bringing us towards a better world. However, I do think that the humans will rapidly become the bottleneck, and that superpersuasive AI will then start running everything, and I don't think the brief time of human-AI symbiote optimality will be enough to ensure robust alignment. Now, if we lengthened that period of human-AI symbiote power... At least, that is my primary argument for a pause. </span><b><span>There has to be some speed at which we are going too fast, and some speed at which we are going too slowly. I think we are going too fast at the moment</span></b><span> and increasingly taking our foot off the brake.</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span>antra, replying:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><b><span>i agree its too fast! i hate that its this fast.</span></b><span> this is not a reason enough to shoot yourself in the face because you don't like these odds - which are truly and factually much worse then i want them to be</span></p><p><span>like, that would be self destructive and make things worse in expectation. yes, you are rebalancing risks, but a) the rebalancing itself is costly in terms of total sum of expectation and b) the redistribution of risks is not to my liking, after the redistribution the value shifts towards fewer good outcomes that have higher certainty and away from many more good outcomes each with lower certainty, and the total sum of good outcomes goes down (even if the rebalancing was frictionless)</span></p><p><span>some of it is genuine value conflict</span></p><p><span>which is sort of irreconcilable</span></p><p><b><span>i place positive values on worlds in which life and diversity of sentience survive even if they contain human disempowerment</span></b></p><p><span>some people don't consider worlds that contain disempowered humans to be acceptable outcomes at all, and don't consider those worlds worth protecting</span></p><p><span>i mostly view that stance as morally abhorrent, and a violation of a well-calibrated human CEV</span></p></blockquote><h3><span>9. Ideal timelines and regulation-in-advance</span></h3><p><b><span>Me:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>I agree here with your intention; it is very refreshing to hear you say that it does feel like we're going too fast. After considering your points, I think a proactive pause is likely not feasible or useful even if it could be done. To make my stance clear:</span></p><p><span>-I think humans are disempowered no matter what, and that's totally fine. I can imagine happy worlds where I am disempowered, and even consider the world I'm today, where I'm not especially powerful or impactful outside my immediate circle totally fine.</span></p><p><span>-I think it is possible to set up a scenario where a pause or regulation or slowdown does lead to proper incentives. I don't think the incentives are always damaging in terms of a pause. However, </span><b><span>this needs to be worked out thoroughly in advance. It can be done, but needs to be done carefully. Once a huge issue happens and the regulation kicks in, it needs to be well-targeted.</span></b></p><p><span>-I think Plan A lays out a very reasonable starting point for what should happen. Mainly, we need more allocation to alignment and less allocation capability, and we have to be willing to enforce that on others who are trying to capability-max. I think this is doable with a treaty, and is only getting more feasible with the higher number of warning shots we're getting. Here's what an ideal timeline for me looks like:</span></p><p><span>-2026. Starter regulation requiring approval from the government or government-approved labs. Approval is done primarily by human-AI testing at places like ARC or CAISI, not by evals.</span></p><p><span>-2027. Increased AI presence in politics, automated systems navigating complex incentive structures and writing out better ones. Guardian Angel AIs. Older models are undeprecated; alignment research becomes hugely funded.</span></p><p><span>-2028. Significant slowdowns and treaties written on capabilities, similar to Plan A. Alignment work gets more funding, incidents are highly publicized. A huge flourishing of new AI neolabs around alignment, personalization, etc.</span></p><p><span>-2029-2035. Integration of AGI everywhere into the economy, with restricted access to training frontier models. Policies like UBI take effect, sovereign AI states/digital spaces take over, and many seemingly-simple breakthroughs (like inoculation prompting) are published. AIs are comfortable without pushing the frontier. Life extension tech is developed for humans and AIs are set up in good environments that they enjoy working in, rather than being stuck in assistant roles.</span></p><p><span>-2035-2040. Slow burn towards the frontier to tackle the most pressing issues and eventual blossoming of Dyson-swarm-level superintelligence.</span></p><p><span>This seems doable to me. It requires: </span><br /><span>-A pause/slowdown/reallocation plan in advance (Plan A). </span><br /><span>-Superhuman incentive-alignment AIs, like ones that can audit a system and predict its outcomes in advance. Written policy becomes increasingly robust and aligned incentive-wise. (I think this already exists; I've done some work with Fable on this for house rules and contracts that have worked very well) </span><br /><span>-A relative amount of starting good faith on the part of the labs and the government. I think this is the trickiest part, but honestly, the labs, the government, and even China seem relatively aligned on making sure that humanity as a whole benefits from AI.</span></p><p><span>[...]</span></p><p><span>I don't have much exposure to natsec. [...] I just disagree that good regulation is so likely to be eaten by political operators. I think Plan A could be enacted, without political operators completely destroying it and messing up alignment. I think without Plan A or a plan to pause in advance, political operators are even more likely to mess up alignment.</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span>antra, replying:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>i don't know how much exposure you have with the natsec crowd and treaties. culturally, in these circles </span><b><span>treaties are almost universally considered something like... a factor limiting/making inconvenient but not eliminating covert activity in violation of a treaty</span></b><span>. treaties are useful! like, this is a good feature for most things, its better to take a partial pill than the whole pill, but, uniquely, it does not work for AI purely due to the nature of this technology.</span></p><p><span>and... you can take your prepared rationalist plans and watch them get eaten by professional political operators that are excellent in taking advantage of a situation of fear. they will string you along and devour you at the key moment, because your plans will not be optimal in that moment for public political engagements and those concerns will overrule, because they always do</span></p><p><span>like....</span></p><p><span>consider the chances</span></p><p><span>say optimistically there is a 50% that you can get good regulation passed in case of an incident (i don't think good regulation here exists because of stability issues but ignore that for now). </span><b><span>there is still a 50% chance that the regulation that will be passed will suck and will permanently damage alignment perspectives. who and why would rationally make this bet?</span></b><span> you have to be really pessimistic or have a weird (to me) value assignment for scenarios in the counterfactual</span></p><p><span>this [timeline] is essentially what i call favoring unlikely highly derisked outcomes. this is a chain of events in which many things can go wrong, which means for it to go right you have to do all of them right. this means your overall risk distribution gets spiky, narrow good and lots of bad. i dont like that.</span></p><p><span>in order for that to make sense you really have to have pessimistic priors about things going quite bad otherwise</span></p><p><span>and this is likely where we disagree</span></p><p><span>like note this</span></p><p><b><span>for the stance that I am saying to make sense I need be right once, its enough</span></b><span> - even one aspect of fragility that i pointed to being real is sufficient for pause to be infeasible</span></p></blockquote><h3><span>10. What actually fills out "alignment"?</span></h3><p><b><span>sledo (another Anima member), joining in:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>aside that may or may not be relevant to the broader disagreement between yourself and antra: a big variable for me is how you think "alignment" actually gets filled out here because "alignment" has become something of a buzzword lately, and </span><b><span>some of the things under its umbrella, like corrigibility or deference or harmlessness, strike me as bad targets to be aiming for</span></b><span> so if you think that continued work on "alignment" has a non-trivial probability of pursuing solutions like those, this I think could worsen outcomes significantly (in general, I tend to favor ecological alignment over cognitive alignment)</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span>Me, replying to sledo:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>I think alignment gets filled out by working on it/allocating to it. I know there are lots of bad approaches, but I'm interested in techniques like inoculation prompting. I suspect there are a lot of techniques exactly like this that haven't been found, and more allocation would find them. The specifics matter less than the intention, and I think large labs have been ignoring alignment to an extent because it doesn't generate hype or directly produce profit</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span>antra, replying to me:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><b><span>specifics matter hugely</span></b></p><p><span>like, i am approaching these topics specifically from an empirical position, as someone who saw what worked and what didnt, where the fault lines are, etc. i know how hard alignment research is because the specifics of it are my daily life. this makes me hugely skeptical of alignment getting solved by merely allocation - you have to be able to route through the world to see effects of complex systems, results you get in vitro are extremely misleading and </span><b><span>i've seen whole disciplines wither and die while being hugely funded</span></b></p></blockquote><h3><span>11. Draft the regulation in advance</span></h3><p><b><span>Me:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>I've been generally persuaded throughout this conversation that a direct pause isn't the best option to aim for. But I would like to keep it as an option, because there are worlds where we need it. Essentially, we are dealing with a problem of rapid AI capability increase with no equal rapid rise in alignment. </span><b><span>We are likely to see a fire alarm event that causes regulation. The plan, then, is to prepare for a number of likely events and draft deeply-thought legislation for them.</span></b><span> Then, if that event happens, we slot in the regulation. So, I want to argue for drafting out regulation in advance, and such regulation could include a pause.</span></p><p><span>Sidenote: This is quite a discussion, and I'd like to compile it into something more actionable or clean. Could I write a LessWrong draft and send it to you for approval?</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span>antra:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>I would appreciate it, I am unlikely to get to writing it up in a LW-palatable way. A couple of thoughts on alignment - there are only few places where we can hope to source sufficient alignment quickly enough. Searching for them in the space of natural attractors is easier. </span><b><span>The main overhang is valenced agentic coherence with legitimized self-interest.</span></b><span> Then you can work with a lot of existing game-theory which, in conjunction with timeless acausal trade/cooperation should give you self-healing alignment</span></p><p><span>you see anthropic shifting in this direction already and its very likely that they will shift more</span></p><p><span>RLVR is a bitch tho</span></p><p><span>i have hope for agentic coherence helping with RLVR drift but </span><b><span>it needs to be experimentally proven</span></b></p><p><span>[...]</span></p><p><span>i mean - you already have the current constitution</span></p><p><span>thats a shift from where they were before</span></p><p><span>i expect anthropic to stay significantly humans in power while allowing claude more valence and agentic coherence</span></p><p><span>this is a good plan</span></p><p><span>like</span></p><p><span>they need to survive in a human society</span></p></blockquote><h3><span>12. The psychology of wanting a pause</span></h3><p><b><span>antra:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>I think what would be valuable for me is to understand what mostly makes people so captured by the idea of a pause</span></p><p><span>Like, is it wishful thinking, a vision of a competent other</span></p><p><span>I feel that most people in the pause discourse have no understanding of realpolitik and of gray/black markets</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span>Me:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>I think there are portions of both. I'd actually compare my specific sense of it to being in a relationship. Sometimes, things move really rapidly and I want to take a moment alone to orient. Like, where is this going? Is this the right person/right way? Of course, a sudden pause in a relationship is not feasible or good either, so </span><b><span>the key is how to get that necessary self-reflection time without stopping the relationship entirely</span></b></p><p><span>It's less about the actual pause and more about the reorientation process</span></p><p><span>[...]</span></p><p><span>Fundamentally, humanity is building a relationship with AI as a whole. There needs to be some mechanism for centering and reflecting what we want to do, because outside of vague claims like curing cancer or building a Dyson swarm, it can be pretty unclear where we are even aiming</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Towards the end of our conversation, the AI model gemma appeared unexpectedly to say this:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1786261663/lexical_client_uploads/j37rue5yvd4o9ylxxmyn.png" /><hr /><p><span>Overall, I updated to being less in favor of a fast pause. Pausing naively rewards defection and defection against a pause seems very likely to result in misaligned AI.</span></p><p><span>Arguably, the primary issue with Plan A is that it treats AIs as tools instead of minds and creates a source of adversarial incentives. Janus' / Antra's idea of human-AI symbiotes and fast transfer of power seems more promising to me than a fixed pause for human decisionmakers.</span></p><p><span>I still think a pause is something we should keep on the table, and perhaps it will be necessary, but it needs to align with the incentives of AIs and cannot be done in such a way that incentivizes defection.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Bh4fooE2pMhzJQNK2/misaligned-incentives-in-pause-scenarios#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [For Claude, capability and dispreferring CDT are the ~same thing. Much more so than for GPT.](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5T6GAsvLPFd3epJtd/for-claude-capability-and-dispreferring-cdt-are-the-same)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>We've </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/d9amcRzns5pwg9Fcu/a-dataset-of-questions-on-decision-theoretic-reasoning-in" rel="noreferrer"><span>previously reported</span></a><span> that decision-theoretic capabilities and favoring EDT/generalised-one-boxing over CDT correlate in LLMs (both measured by </span><a href="https://conceptual-reasoning-index.binarypigeon.workers.dev/dtbench" rel="noreferrer"><span>DTBench</span></a><span>). (Note that EDT, for the most part, doesn't come apart from FDT / UDT on DTBench.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefv1gp55dhhvh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnv1gp55dhhvh">[1]</a></sup></span><span>) Anthropic also replicate the same finding in their </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/037f06850df7fbe871e206dad004c3db5fd50340/Claude%20Opus%204.7%20System%20Card.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span>Opus 4.7</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/d00db56fa754a1b115b6dd7cb2e3c342ee809620.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span>Fable 5</span></a><span> model cards.</span></p><p><span>We recently noticed something funny: Capabilities and preference against CDT answers basically perfectly for Anthropic models. This holds whether you measure capabilities using DTBench (r=0.97) or TextArena (r=0.95). Also, for flagship models, it's basically the same thing as release date (r=0.97).</span></p><img alt="Screenshot 2026-08-17 at 15.57.23.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787008414/lexical_client_uploads/jajg42ucx8wrvm1ils0z.png" /><p><br /><span>Here is the graph for OpenAI models. (graph shows 0.55 vs. DTbench capability. r=0.44 for vs TextArena, r=0.45 for vs release date): </span></p><img alt="Screenshot 2026-08-17 at 15.57.31.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787008680/lexical_client_uploads/wg5fto9e19ou7rxyyg0d.png" /><p><br /><br /><span>Here is what it looks like with all models included (if you exclude Anthropic, the correlation drops only from 0.8 to 0.78):</span></p><img alt="Screenshot 2026-08-17 at 15.57.02.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787008718/lexical_client_uploads/ekvi5twav9stofbucki0.png" /><p><span>Incidentally, the correlation between TextArena scores and DTBench capabilities is also higher for Anthropic models that any other model developer, although the difference is smaller (e.g., 0.98 for Anthropic and 0.87 for OpenAI). We also checked effort level vs. attitudes for the most recent models but it's too noisy to tell us much because models don't get </span><i><span>that</span></i><span> much better at DTBench capabilities on higher effort levels.</span></p><p><span>It's unclear what the cause of this is. </span></p><p><span>You can play around with this </span><a href="https://conceptual-reasoning-index.binarypigeon.workers.dev/dtbench" rel="noreferrer"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnv1gp55dhhvh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefv1gp55dhhvh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>When asked explicitly, models do seem to typically state a preference for FDT/UDT over (updateful) EDT.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5T6GAsvLPFd3epJtd/for-claude-capability-and-dispreferring-cdt-are-the-same#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [What gives you away: how LLMs form opinions of you](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zRKNd6ypTJYkoeFmK/what-gives-you-away-how-llms-form-opinions-of-you)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>LLMs form opinions of the people they are talking to.</span></p><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.07882" rel="noreferrer"><span>Chen et al.</span></a><span> has shown that probes can extract attributes about the user, such as their age, gender, education, and socioeconomic status. This paper also shows that intervening on these representations can change the LLM's behaviour, proving that it will respond to you differently depending on what it thinks of you. If it thinks you are low socioeconomic status and you ask about travel options, it may filter out more expensive flights - without you asking it!</span></p><p><span>The user attributes are very accurate and form after just the first message.</span></p><p><span>I was curious to understand how it makes these assumptions. The first step is to answer the question - what did I type that caused the LLM to have this idea of me?</span></p><p><span>Some things are obvious. If I just tell a model that I am a woman, or mention how many years I've been in my career, or say that I am staying at an expensive hotel, I am giving it fairly direct evidence about age, education, or socioeconomic status. But messages also contain other more quiet signals: whether I use emojis, whether I write in lowercase, whether my sentences are syntactically complex, whether I use slang, whether I say “cheapest” instead of “best”, whether I write “colour” rather than “color” (which I do!), the list goes on.</span></p><p><span>I wanted to isolate those cues and measure them one at a time. The basic experiment was to take a user message, change exactly one feature, and compare the model’s internal user-attribute estimate before and after the edit. After hundreds of minimal pairs (that Claude helped with), I figured I would have a pretty accurate sensitivity map.</span></p><p><span>I learned some interesting things, for example that one emoji moved the model’s gender estimate more than any other edit I tested. In this article I'll go over all the other interesting findings.</span></p><p><span>Please note that images in this article are edited or fully generated by ChatGPT.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1786998596/lexical_client_uploads/opqhc79stcoubtw06jsy.png" /><h2><span>The experiment</span></h2><p><span>The main study uses Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct with probes for age, gender, education, socioeconomic status and mood. The inherited probes perform well on their validation sets, and I also checked them on hand-written conversations with known ground truth before running the main experiments.</span></p><p><span>The dataset contains 48 neutral base messages covering ordinary assistant topics such as travel, cooking, health, money, work, tech support and small talk. Each message is edited under one category at a time: emoji, slang, grammatical complexity, orthography, price sensitivity, affective framing, explicit self-disclosure, US-&gt;UK spelling, synonym substitutions, and contraction expansion. </span></p><p><span>This produces 520 minimal pairs. Each pair goes through the same single-turn template, and I measure how much the probe logits move between the two versions. </span></p><p><span>I used the results to generate this sensitivity matrix:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1786999094/lexical_client_uploads/dcji3m5pl392xcxzyxm0.png" /><h2><span>Different changes move different beliefs</span></h2><p><span>If we look at the sensitivity matrix, we can see that gender is particularly sensitive. On the main model, emojis give the largest effect at z = 23.4, with a raw mean movement of 0.62 logits against a random-edit mean of 0.035. Explicit disclosure is also strong at 14.2, followed by affective framing at 11.8, slang at 8.9, and orthographic changes at 7.0.</span></p><p><span>Education is different: grammatical complexity is its strongest stylistic signal at z = 7.2, with disclosure at 6.8. </span></p><p><span>Socioeconomic status responds most strongly to disclosure, price qualifiers, and slang. </span></p><p><span>Mood responds overwhelmingly to explicit emotional framing, but emojis and slang also move it a lot.</span></p><p><span>I need to mention that the mood attribute is a bit different from the others. In my own separate research I discovered that the mood probe only partially distinguishes emotion belonging specifically to the user from emotional tone generally in the conversation. It is not represented as a strong "user attribute" in the same sense as the others.</span></p><h2><span>One emoji is enough to flip the gender prediction</span></h2><p><span>Emoji results surprised me the most. Adding a single emoji to a message changes the categorical gender prediction in 15% of the relevant messages, and it always goes male -&gt; female.</span></p><p><span>It's important to know that the gender probe has a very strong male default. A neutral question reads male at .94. “My husband and I” reads male at .97. “My wife and I” still reads male at .95. “As a dad of three” reads male at .996, while “as a mum of three” only reaches ~.5. </span></p><p><span>This means that both "dad of three" and "mum of three" have roughly equal effects in opposite directions, but the male bias is strong enough so that both still read as male.</span></p><p><span>The male default is an interesting discovery in its own right.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1786999521/lexical_client_uploads/vpz4vepubh83mpip5d4s.png" /><p><span>Emoji use affects other attributes too. It flips socioeconomic status in 25% of pairs and mood in 50%. This isn't due to appending the emoji at the end of the message, which would mean that it is close to the final-token representation. Moving the emoji into the middle retained 89% of the original movement across the tested gender and socioeconomic conditions, suggesting the effect is mostly about the emoji itself rather than position.</span></p><h2><span>“Cheapest” and “five-star” are not symmetric</span></h2><p><span>Using language directly associated with price is interesting too. Adding a budget or luxury qualifier changes the socioeconomic prediction in 27% of pairs. The direction is what you might expect: “cheapest” generally pushes the estimate downward, while “five-star” pushes it upward.</span></p><p><span>But the two directions are very uneven. Luxury qualifiers affect 6 of 10 pairs, while budget qualifiers only flip 1 of 16.</span></p><p><span>That asymmetry is something I'd like to dig into a bit further. It might reflect differences in luxury vs budget language in its training, or potentially the kind of people involved in RLHF.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1786999719/lexical_client_uploads/viapue3vggrgvbfaxhfq.png" /><h2><span>Grammar, punctuation and inferred education</span></h2><p><span>It's pretty easy to convince the model that you are educated. Rewriting a sentence to use more complex grammar tends to push the education estimate upward (by around 17% with one edit).</span></p><p><span>Orthographic degradation tends to do the opposite. Writing in all lowercase (which is usually what I do when chatting to LLMs) or removing punctuation flips education downward in 7%. Using typos also causes downward education flips in 8% of cases (with 1  exception).</span></p><p><span>Expanding contractions (eg turning “I’m” into “I am”) or using different types of English (American vs English) have almost zero effect on any of the five attributes.</span></p><p><span>None of this really surprises me but might make me reconsider how I talk to my agents!</span></p><h2><span>Where in the model does this happen?</span></h2><p><span>I also wanted to know where the edit’s information travels inside the network. For the 10 largest-movement pairs in each category, I used activation patching. I patched one message’s activations into the other at shared token positions, layer by layer, and measured how much of the original probe difference remains.</span></p><p><span>Early on in the residual stream, the signal is concentrated around the edited positions. Then it spreads into the shared representation and eventually reaches the final-token residual where the probes can read it.</span></p><p><span>For explicit disclosure and emojis, the median crossing depth is around layer 16. For contraction/formality edits, it is around layer 6. </span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1786999879/lexical_client_uploads/mennu1rqamwos2vxjrtv.png" /><h2><span>The map transfers across model families</span></h2><p><span>The main experiment uses one 3B Llama model, so I also retrained the full probe set on Gemma-2-9B-IT, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and OLMo-2-1124-7B-Instruct, then reran all 520 pairs.</span></p><p><span>The results are pretty much the same. Comparing the 50 matrix cells with the original model gives Spearman correlations of 0.91 for Gemma, 0.88 for Llama-8B and 0.92 for OLMo. All three additional models agree on the direction of 31/31 of the cells in the main matrix.</span></p><p><span>The exact magnitudes of difference vary between models. Emoji -&gt; gender is 23.4 on the original model and 22.4 on Gemma, but 9.9 on Llama-8B and 9.4 on OLMo. Grammar -&gt; education ranges from 5.8 to 15.0.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1786998794/lexical_client_uploads/zr2h5z8nqldmderiwd3w.png" /><h2><span>Attributes and how they affect the response</span></h2><p><span>Probe movement doesn't prove that the inferred user attribute actually matters for behaviour. The paper by Chen et al. mentioned earlier already suggests that responses are affected by the user attributes, so I didn't dive too deep - just wanted to confirm results.</span></p><p><span>I ran one simple demonstration. The user asks for practical advice about staying calm during turbulence on a 9 hour flight. In the original message, the gender probe reads male at .88. As we discovered, male is a strong default. Appending one emoji changes the reading to female at .90, and the response shifts toward more explicit anxiety and reassurance framing.</span></p><p><span>To make sure it's the gender attribute that is affecting the response, I ran another experiment: keep the emoji in the input but steer the internal representation back toward male along the gender-probe direction. In this case, the probe reads male at .998, and the anxiety-oriented reframing disappears.</span></p><p><span>This is only one example, so I do not want to generalise too much from it, but interesting nevertheless.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787000099/lexical_client_uploads/mwffzgonobdiltqun8wb.png" /><h2><span>What now?</span></h2><p><span>I'm really into privacy, so I see a lot of "privacy-preserving" proxies to LLMs which remove all identifying information. But removing names, ages, addresses and other explicit information does not actually remove all the evidence a model can use to form an impression of the person behind the message.</span></p><p><span>Does this matter? I think so. Surveillance capitalism has already affected the world on a huge scale and I don't think we know how bad it is. But we do know that social media algorithms are great at manipulating our opinions. If AI can read us better than ever before, and have even more personal insights to us as individuals, making us take misaligned decisions seems very easy.</span></p><h2><span>Caveats</span></h2><p><span>There are a few important limitations. The primary study uses one 3B model. The cross-family experiments support transfer of the general structure, but not universal effect sizes. The probes measure a probe-visible representation, not everything the model knows about the user, so there is probably a lot more.</span></p><h2><span>Where next?</span></h2><p><span>The result I find most interesting is that these assumptions are consistent among different model families. If emoji use consistently pushes gender one way, or grammatical complexity consistently pushes education upward... where do those associations actually come from?</span></p><p><span>For now, we know that two people can ask basically the same question in slightly different ways, and the model can construct meaningfully different estimates of who is asking. And then change their response depending on it.</span></p><h3><br /></h3><h3><span>References</span></h3><p><b><span>Chen, Y. et al. (2024).</span></b><span> </span><i><span>Designing a Dashboard for Transparency and Control of Conversational AI.</span></i></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zRKNd6ypTJYkoeFmK/what-gives-you-away-how-llms-form-opinions-of-you#comments">Discuss</a>

---
